where
|h_{i}|=n
for
i=1
,
2
,
3
, and
|s_{1}|=|s_{2}|=n-1
, which can be visualized as a matrix

\begin{bmatrix}h_{1}&&\\
&h_{2}&\\
s_{2}&s_{1}&h_{3}\end{bmatrix}.

Put
H^{\prime}=d(H)\in\mathrm{Hom}^{n+1}(F,F^{\prime})
. Then
H^{\prime}
corresponds to the matrix:

\begin{bmatrix}-d(h_{1})&&\\
&-d(h_{2})&\\
d(s_{2})+j^{\prime}h_{1}-(-1)^{n}h_{3}j&d(s_{1})+f^{\prime}h_{2}-(-1)^{n}h_{3}f&d(h_{3})\end{bmatrix}.

The composition map is defined by matrix multiplication.
Note that we have a canonical restriction dg functor

\mathrm{Str}:\mathrm{Mor}(\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{A}))\rightarrow\mathrm{Cosp}(\mathcal{A}),\;\;\hbox to186.76pt{\vbox to52.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 93.37971pt\lower-26.21582pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-93.37971pt}{-19.83531pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

Dually, we have the dg category
\mathrm{Sp}(\mathcal{A})
of
homotopy spans
in
\mathcal{A}
and a canonical restriction dg functor
\mathrm{Str}^{\prime}:\mathrm{Mor}(\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{A}))\rightarrow\mathrm{Sp}(\mathcal{A})
.

Definition 3.24
.

The category
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Cosp}}(\mathcal{A})
of homotopy cospans in
\mathcal{A}
is defined as

H^{0}(\mathrm{Cosp}(\mathcal{A})).

Dually, the category
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Sp}}(\mathcal{A})
of homotopy spans in
\mathcal{A}
is defined as

H^{0}(\mathrm{Sp}(\mathcal{A})).

By Theorem
3.16
, we have equivalences of categories

\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Sp}}(\mathcal{A})\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathrm{rep}(k\mathrm{Sp},\mathcal{A})

and

\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Cosp}}(\mathcal{A})\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathrm{rep}(k\mathrm{Cosp},\mathcal{A}).

Definition 3.25
.

An object
S
in
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Cosp}}(\mathcal{A})
, as given by (
3.23.1
), is said to admit a homotopy pullback if there is an object
X
in
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Sq}}(\mathcal{A})

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

which is a homotopy pullback square.
In this case, the object
X
is called the homotopy pullback of
S
.

Now, we define the notions of homotopy kernel (cokernel) of an object in
\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{A})
.
Recall the morphism category
\mathrm{Mor}
defined in Subsection
2.3

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1,}

and the dg category
\mathcal{B}
defined in (
3.13.1
)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

where
|f|=|j|=0
, and
d(f)=d(j)=0
,
jf=0
.
Let
i:k\mathrm{Mor}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}
be the inclusion dg functor which sends object
0
to
0
and object
1
to
1
.
Let
i^{\prime}:k\mathrm{Mor}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}
be the inclusion dg functor which sends object
0
to
1
and object
1
to
2
.
Let
\mathrm{Res}:\mathrm{Fun}_{{A}_{\infty}}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A})\rightarrow\mathrm{Fun}_{{A}_{\infty}}(k\mathrm{Mor},\mathcal{A})\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{A})
(resp.
\mathrm{Res}^{\prime}
) be the restriction functor along
i
(resp.
i^{\prime}
).

Definition 3.26
.

By homotopy kernel of an object
j:B\rightarrow C
in
\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{A})
, we mean a homotopy left exact squence
X

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

Sometimes, we say
f:A\rightarrow B
or
A
is the homotopy kernel of
j
.

Dually, we define the homotopy cokernel of an object in
\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{A})
.

In Definition
3.25
,
\mathrm{Str}(X)
is stated to be equal to
S
, and in Definition
3.26
,
\mathrm{Res}^{\prime}(X)
is defined to be equal to
j
. Meanwhile, in Definition
3.8
,
LX
is asserted to be isomorphic to
S
, cf. Remark
3.9
.
The above definitions are compatible with each other, due to the following observation:

Lemma 3.27
.

Let
j:B\rightarrow C
be an object in
\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{A})
.
Consider a morphism
\theta
from
j^{\prime}:B^{\prime}\rightarrow C^{\prime}
to
j
in
Z^{0}(\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{A}))
as follows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\mathrlap{.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

Suppose we are given an object

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}}

in
\mathcal{H}_{3t}(\mathcal{A})
.
Then we have the following morphism
\mu
in
Z^{0}\mathrm{Fun}_{{A}_{\infty}}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A})
which restricts to the morphism
\theta

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\mathrlap{.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{1}f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{2}h^{\prime}-s_{2}f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

Remark 3.28
.

In Lemma
3.27
, the homotopy class of the morphism
\mu
is an isomorphism in
H^{0}(\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{A}))
if and only if the homotopy class of the morphism
\theta
is an isomorphism in
\mathcal{H}_{3t}(\mathcal{A})
, cf. Proposition
3.15
.

3.3.
Some diagram lemmas

In the rest of this section, we prove some basic diagram lemmas that will used in the sequel.

Let us recall the universal property of homotopy left exact sequences.
Suppose we have 3-term h-complexes
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
with
X_{2}
being homotopy left exact.
Then each morphism
\theta:\mathrm{Res}^{\prime}(X_{1})\rightarrow\mathrm{Res}^{\prime}(X_{2})
in
H^{0}(\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{A}))
extends uniquely to a morphism
\mu:X_{1}\rightarrow X_{2}
in
\mathcal{H}_{3t}(\mathcal{A})
such that
\theta=\mathrm{Res}^{\prime}(\mu)
.
Indeed, we have the following

Lemma 3.29
.

Suppose we have 3-term h-complexes
X_{i}
in
\mathcal{A}
,
i=1
,
2
, of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{i}}

where
X_{2}
is homotopy left exact. Then any morphism in
Z^{0}(\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{A}))

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{2}}

extends to a morphism in
Z^{0}(\mathrm{Fun}_{\infty}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A}))

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{2}\mathrlap{.}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{2}}

Proof.

Put
u=h_{2}f_{1}
and
v=h_{3}h_{1}-s_{2}f_{1}
. Then the pair
(u,v)
satisfies
d(v)=-h_{3}j_{1}f_{1}-(j_{2}h_{2}-h_{3}j_{1})f_{1}=-j_{2}h_{2}f_{1}=-j_{2}u
.

Since
X_{2}
is homotopy left exact, we have a closed morphism
a:A_{1}\rightarrow A_{2}
of degree
0
such that there exists a pair
(-s_{1},-t)
where
s_{1}
is a morphism from
A_{1}
to
B_{2}
of degree
-1
,
t
is a morphism from
A_{1}
to
C_{2}
of degree
-2
satisfying
u-f_{2}a=d(-s_{1})
and
v-h_{2}a=-d(-t)-j(-s_{1})
. So the 6-tuple
(a,b,h_{3},s_{1},s_{2},t)
is a morphism from
X_{1}
to
X_{2}
in
Z^{0}(\mathrm{Fun}_{\infty}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A}))
.
∎

Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be an object in
\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{A})
and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\wedge}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\wedge}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\wedge}}

the associated object
S
in the category
\mathrm{rep}(k\mathrm{Cosp},\mathcal{A})
.
Recall that we have a fully faithful functor
\mathcal{H}_{3t}(\mathcal{A})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{rep}(k\mathrm{Sq},\mathcal{A})
.
Then a homotopy kernel of the object
f
, which is in the category
\mathcal{H}_{3t}(\mathcal{A})
, can be identified with a homotopy pullback of the cospan
S
, which is in the category
\mathrm{rep}(k\mathrm{Sq},\mathcal{A})
.
Hence by Proposition
3.10
, the homotopy kernel of an object in
\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{A})
is unique up to a unique isomorphism if it exists in
\mathcal{H}_{3t}(\mathcal{A})
.
Similarly, the homotopy pullback of an object in
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Cosp}}(\mathcal{A})
is unique up to a unique isomorphism if it exists in
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Sq}}(\mathcal{A})
.

\mathcal{H}_{3t}(\mathcal{A})

.

Following the preceding discussion, the two morphisms
\mathrm{Res}(\alpha)
,
\mathrm{Res}^{\prime}(\alpha)
yield two objects in
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Sq}}(\mathcal{A})
, denoted by the same notations for clarity.

Definition 3.30
.

As above, let
\alpha:X\rightarrow X^{\prime}
be a morphism in
\mathcal{H}_{3t}(\mathcal{A})
.
We call
\mathrm{Res}(\alpha)\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Sq}}(\mathcal{A})
the restriction of
\alpha:X\rightarrow X^{\prime}
to
f:A\rightarrow B
.
Similarly, we call
\mathrm{Res}^{\prime}(\alpha)
the restriction of
\alpha:X\rightarrow X^{\prime}
to
j:B\rightarrow C
.

Proposition 3.31
.

Suppose we have objects
X
and
X^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{H}_{3t}(\mathcal{A})
of the following form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

where, for
X^{\prime}
, we add a prime symbol superscript to each term.
Let
\alpha:X\rightarrow X^{\prime}
be a morphism in
\mathcal{H}_{3t}(\mathcal{A})
as in (
3.29.1
).

1)

Suppose
h_{2}:A_{2}\rightarrow A_{2}^{\prime}
is a homotopy equivalence.
Let
X^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Sq}}(\mathcal{A})
be the restriction of
\alpha
to
f:A_{0}\rightarrow A_{1}
.
Then the following statements hold:

(a)

If
X^{\prime}
is homotopy left exact, then
X
is a homotopy left exact if and only if
X^{\prime\prime}
is a homotopy pullback square.

(b)

If
X^{\prime\prime}
is a homotopy pushout square, then
X
is homotopy right exact if and only if so is
X^{\prime}
.

2)

Suppose
h_{0}:A_{0}\rightarrow A_{0}^{\prime}
is a homotopy equivalence.
Let
X^{\prime\prime\prime}
be the restriction of
\alpha
to
j:A_{1}\rightarrow A_{2}
. Then the following statements hold:

(a)

If
X
is homotopy right exact, then
X^{\prime}
is homotopy right exact if and only if
X^{\prime\prime\prime}
is a homotopy pushout square.

(b)

If
X^{\prime\prime\prime}
is a homotopy pullback square, then
X
is homotopy left exact if and only if so is
X^{\prime}
.

Proof.

We prove 1) and then 2) follows by duality.
For simplicity, we omit the symbol
∧
of the representable dg modules
A^{\wedge}
.
Since
h_{2}
is a homotopy equivalence, we may assume
h_{2}
is the identity of
{A_{2}}
.
The morphism
\alpha
is given by the following diagram in
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V=\Sigma^{-1}\mathrm{Cone}(j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\prime}=\Sigma^{-1}\mathrm{Cone}(j^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2},}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{bmatrix}s_{1}\\
t\end{bmatrix}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u=\begin{bmatrix}f\\
h\end{bmatrix}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[}-1{,}0{]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{bmatrix}h_{1}\;\;0\\
-s_{2}\;\;1\end{bmatrix}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{\prime}=\begin{bmatrix}f^{\prime}\\
h^{\prime}\end{bmatrix}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[}-1{,}0{]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}}

where diagonal maps are the homotopies making the diagram commutative in
\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{A})
.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

We have a morphism from the sequence in blue to the mapping triangle of
v
as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma V_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Cone}(v)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma V_{3}\mathrlap{.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{bmatrix}0\;\;0\\
h_{1}\;\;0\\
-s_{2}\;\;1\end{bmatrix}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{bmatrix}0\;\;0\;\;0\\
1\;\;0\;\;0\\
0\;\;1\;\;0\end{bmatrix}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[}0{,}1{]^{\intercal}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[}1{,}0{]}}

By direct inspection, we see that
\mathrm{Cone}(v)
is isomorphic to the mapping cone of

\mathrm{Cone}(\mathrm{id}_{\Sigma^{-1}A_{1}})\rightarrow W.

The map
\theta
is the canonical inclusion from
W
to this cone.
It is straightforward to check that the rightmost square commutes and the middle square commutes up to homotopy.
Now we see that the composition of the canonical maps
A_{0}\xrightarrow{[f,h_{0},s_{1}]^{\intercal}}V_{3}
and
v:V_{3}\rightarrow V
is homotopic to the canonical map
u:A_{0}\rightarrow V
.

(a) Since
X^{\prime}
is homotopy left exact, the map
u^{\prime}:A_{0}^{\prime}\rightarrow V^{\prime}
induces a quasi-isomorphism

\tau_{\leq 0}\mathrm{RHom}(A,\Sigma^{-i}A_{0}^{\prime})\rightarrow\tau_{\leq 0}\mathrm{RHom}(A,\Sigma^{-i}V^{\prime})

for each
A^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
.
Thus the induced map

\tau_{\leq 0}\mathrm{Hom}(A^{\prime},V_{3})\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}(A^{\prime},V)

is a quasi-isomorphism for each
A^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
.
Therefore
X
is homotopy left exact if and only if
X^{\prime\prime}
is homotopy cartesian.

(b) Put
U_{3}=\mathrm{Cone}((f,h_{0})^{\intercal})
.
Consider the following diagrams

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Since
X^{\prime\prime}
is homotopy cocartesian, the canonical map
U_{3}\rightarrow A_{1}^{\prime}
induces a quasi-isomorphism

\tau_{\leq 0}\mathrm{RHom}(A_{1}^{\prime},A^{\prime})\rightarrow\tau_{\leq 0}\mathrm{RHom}(U_{3},A^{\prime})

for each
A^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
. This implies that the canonical map
U\rightarrow U^{\prime}
induces a quasi-isomorphism

\tau_{\leq 0}\mathrm{RHom}(U^{\prime},A^{\prime})\rightarrow\tau_{\leq 0}\mathrm{RHom}(U,A^{\prime})

for each
A^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
. From the rightmost square, we infer that
X
is homotopy right exact if and only if so is
X^{\prime}
.
∎

Corollary 3.32
.

Suppose we have the following diagram in
\mathcal{A}
with lower square being homotopy pullback and the upper square an object in
\mathrm{Mor}(\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{A}))
.
Then the outer square (i.e. the square on the right hand side) is homotopy pullback if and only if so is the upper square.

Proof.

We may assume that the dg category
\mathcal{A}
admits a zero object and direct sums.
Note that direct sums of homotopy cartesian squares remain homotopy cartesian.
Also, note that squares of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

are trivially homotopy cartesian.

The statement follows by applying Proposition
3.31
to one of the following two diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}\oplus A_{5}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{6}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}\oplus A_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{4}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{4}\oplus A_{5}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{6}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}\oplus A_{5}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{6}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime}g+j^{\prime}h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}

where
r=\begin{bmatrix}j\;\;0\\
0\;\;\mathrm{id}\end{bmatrix}
,
s=\begin{bmatrix}h\\
0\end{bmatrix}
,
u=\begin{bmatrix}f\\
g^{\prime}g\end{bmatrix}
,
v={[}j^{\prime}j{,}-k^{\prime}{]}
,
w=\begin{bmatrix}f^{\prime}\\
g^{\prime}\end{bmatrix}
and
z={[}j^{\prime}{,}-k^{\prime}{]}
.
∎

Suppose we have a homotopy pullback square
X_{2}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

and an object

in

where
g
is a morphism from
D
to
E
.
Then the triple
(c,gd,s_{3})
satisfies
d(s_{3})=hgd-fc=-[-h,\;f]\begin{bmatrix}gd\\
c\end{bmatrix}
, and thus, by Lemma
3.20
, we have a morphism
a:A\rightarrow B
such that there exists a triple
(s,s_{1},t)
such that

\begin{bmatrix}gd\\
c\end{bmatrix}-\begin{bmatrix}e\\
b\end{bmatrix}a=\begin{bmatrix}d(s_{1})\\
d(s)\end{bmatrix}

and

s_{3}-s_{2}a=-d(t)-[-h,\;f]\begin{bmatrix}s_{1}\\
s\end{bmatrix}.

So we have the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

where the left hand square
X_{1}
is an object in
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Sq}}(\mathcal{A})
and
(-s,0,-t)
serves as a homotopy between
X_{3}
and the composition of
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
, all regarded as morphisms in
Z^{0}(\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{A}))
.
So we have

Corollary 3.33
.

Keep the notations as above.
If we have homotopy pullback squares
X_{2}
and
X_{3}
, then there exists a pair
(a,s_{1})
such that
X_{1}
is also homotopy pullback and that the composition of
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
is homotopic to
X_{3}
, all regarded as morphisms in
Z^{0}(\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{A}))
.

Proposition 3.34
.

We have a homotopy right exact sequence

and a morphism

(3.34.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{8.01212pt}{-25.48367pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;.

Consider the morphism

(h,-j,0):M=\mathrm{Cone}(A\xrightarrow{[f,\alpha]^{\intercal}}B\oplus A^{\prime})\rightarrow C.

Since
X^{\prime\prime}
is the homotopy pushout of
S
, we have a morphism
j^{\prime}:B^{\prime}\rightarrow C
such that there exists a graded morphism
(-t,s_{2},h^{\prime}):M\rightarrow C
of degree
-1
such that

d(-t,s_{2},h^{\prime})=(-j^{\prime}s_{1}+h,-j+j^{\prime}h_{1},-j^{\prime}f^{\prime}).

So we have

(-d(t)-h^{\prime}\alpha-s_{2}f,d(s_{2}),d(h^{\prime}))=(-j^{\prime}s_{1}+h,-j+j^{\prime}h_{1},-j^{\prime}f^{\prime}).

Thus we have a morphism
\mu:X\rightarrow X^{\prime}
of the form (
3.34.1
), where
X^{\prime}
is the 3-term h-complex on the second row.
By Proposition
3.31
, we deduce that
X^{\prime}
is homotopy right exact and that if both
X
and
X^{\prime}
are homotopy short exact sequences, then
X^{\prime\prime}
is homotopy bicartesian.
∎

The following is a direct consequence of Lemma
3.27
and Proposition
3.34
.

the object
f
in
\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{A})
has a homotopy cokernel if and only if the object
k
has a homotopy cokernel.
If this is the case, there exists a morphism in
\mathcal{H}_{3t}(\mathcal{A})
from the homotopy cokernel of
f
to that of
k
such that its restriction to the third term is the identity, and its restriction to
f:A\rightarrow B
is isomorphic to
X
.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;.

We still denote by
X
the associated morphism from
f:A\rightarrow B
to
k:C\rightarrow D
given by the homotopy cartesian square
X
. Then there exists a morphism
\beta:f^{\prime}\rightarrow f
in
H^{0}(\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{A}))
such that
X\circ\beta=\alpha
and that
\beta
restricts to
\mathrm{id}_{B}
.

Proof.

Consider the morphism

A^{\prime}\xrightarrow{[f^{\prime},-g^{\prime},s^{\prime}]^{\intercal}}M=\Sigma^{-1}\mathrm{Cone}(B\oplus C\xrightarrow{[j,k]}D).

Since
X
is homotopy cartesian, we have a morphism
r:A^{\prime}\rightarrow A
such that there exists a graded morphism
[u,v,w]^{\intercal}:A^{\prime}\rightarrow M
of degree
-1
, such that

d([u,v,w]^{\intercal})=[f,-g,s]^{\intercal}\circ r-[f^{\prime},-g^{\prime},s^{\prime}]^{\intercal}.

So we have
d(u)=fr-f^{\prime}
,
d(v)=g^{\prime}-gr
and
d(w)=-ju-kv+s^{\prime}-sr
.

We have the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\mathrlap{.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}

So we have a morphism
\beta:f^{\prime}\rightarrow f
in
H^{0}(\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{A}))
as follows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

such that the composition of
\beta
with
X
is homotopic to
\alpha
.
∎

We also have the following direct consequence of Lemma
3.27
and Proposition
3.34
.

Lemma 3.37
.

If the homotopy cospan
L

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}

admits a homotopy pullback, then we have the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}}

where

a)

The left bottom square is a homotopy pullback square.

b)

The 3-term h-complex on the leftmost column is a homotopy left exact sequence.

c)

The 3-term h-complex in the middle row is homotopy left exact. It is homotopy short exact if the homotopy pullback of
L
is homotopy short exact.

d)

The 6-tuple
(\mathrm{id}_{A},w,f^{\prime},s^{\prime},s,t)
is a morphism in
\mathcal{H}_{3t}(\mathcal{A})
from the 3-term h-complex in the first column to that in the second column.

e)

We have
h^{\prime\prime\prime}=j^{\prime}s+h^{\prime}v
and the 6-tuple
(v,j,\mathrm{id}_{D},s,0,0)
yields a morphism in
\mathcal{H}_{3t}(\mathcal{A})
from the 3-term h-complex in the second row to that in the bottom row.

Recall the diagram functor
\mathrm{Dia}:H^{0}(\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{A}))\to\mathrm{Mor}(H^{0}(\mathcal{A}))
which is an epivalence.
By definition, an object
f:A\to B
of
\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{A})
is a homotopy equivalence if
\mathrm{Dia}(f)
is an isomorphism in
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
.
Therefore, an object
f:A\to B
of
\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{A})
is a homotopy equivalence if and only if
this holds for any object
f^{\prime}:A^{\prime}\to B^{\prime}
such that
\mathrm{Dia}(f)
and
\mathrm{Dia}(f^{\prime})
are
isomorphic in
\mathrm{Mor}(H^{0}(\mathcal{A}))
.
It is straightforward to verify the following lemma.

.

of
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Sq}}(\mathcal{A})
which is homotopy cartesian.
If the morphism
j
is a homotopy equivalence, then so is the morphism
g
.

4.
Exact dg categories

In this section, we introduce the notion of exact structure on an additive dg category,
based on the notion of homotopy short exact sequence, cf. Definition
4.1
.
We then establish that for an exact dg category
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})
, there exists a canonical extriangulated structure
(H^{0}(\mathcal{A}),\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
on
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
, cf. Theorem
4.26
.
Furthermore, we demonstrate, in Theorem
4.33
, the existence of a canonical bijection between the lattice of exact substructures of
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})
and the lattice of closed subbifunctors of
\mathbb{E}
.

4.1.
Exact dg structure

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a dg category over
k
which is
additive
, i.e.
H^{0}{\mathcal{A}}
is additive.
For an object
f:A\rightarrow B
in
H^{0}(\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{A}))
, we denote by
[f]
its isomorphism class and by
\overline{f}
the corresponding morphism in
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
, i.e. the object
\mathrm{Dia}(f)
in
\mathrm{Mor}(H^{0}(\mathcal{A}))
.
Recall that we have an epivalence
\mathrm{Dia}:H^{0}(\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{A}))\rightarrow\mathrm{Mor}(H^{0}(\mathcal{A}))
.
Therefore, if a property of objects in
H^{0}(\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{A}))
is stable under isomorphisms, we will also say that the corresponding objects in
\mathrm{Mor}(H^{0}(\mathcal{A}))
have the same property.
Note that if the object
f:A\rightarrow B
is isomorphic to
f^{\prime}:A^{\prime}\rightarrow B^{\prime}
and
g:B\rightarrow C
is isomorphic to
g^{\prime}:B^{\prime}\rightarrow C^{\prime}
in
H^{0}(\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{A}))
, it is not necessarily true that
gf

is isomorphic to
g^{\prime}f^{\prime}
.
But this will not cause confusion because in what follows, we consider objects in
H^{0}(\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{A}))
up to isomorphism.
For example, when we say “compositions of deflations are deflations”,
it means that
gf
is a deflation whenever
g
and
f
are deflations and then
g^{\prime}f^{\prime}
is also a deflation,
although
g^{\prime}f^{\prime}
and
gf
may not be isomorphic.

Definition 4.1
.

An exact structure on
\mathcal{A}
is a class
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{3t}(\mathcal{A})
stable under isomorphisms, consisting of homotopy short exact sequences (called conflations)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

where
i
is called an inflation and
p
is called a deflation, such that the following axioms are satisfied:

Ex0

\mathrm{id}_{0}
is a deflation.

Ex1

Compositions of deflations are deflations.

Ex2

Given a deflation
p:B\rightarrow C
and any map
c:C^{\prime}\rightarrow C
in
Z^{0}(\mathcal{A})
, the cospan

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}

admits a homotopy pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

and
{p^{\prime}}
is also a deflation.

\mathrm{Ex}2^{op}

Given an inflation
i:A\rightarrow B
and any map
a:A\rightarrow A^{\prime}
in
Z^{0}(\mathcal{A})
, the span

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}

admits a homotopy pushout

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}

and
{i^{\prime}}
is also an inflation.

We call
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})
, or simply
\mathcal{A}
, an exact dg category.

Remark 4.2
.

Let us emphasize that our notion of exact dg category is completely different from Positselski’s notion
of exact DG-category
[
37
]
. For example, an exact structure in our sense can be
transported along a quasi-equivalence, cf. Remark
4.5
b), which is not at all the case for exact structures in the sense of Positselski. His principal motivation is to axiomatise
situations where we have a strongly pretriangulated dg category
\mathcal{A}
whose category
Z^{0}(\mathcal{A})

is moreover endowed with a Quillen exact structure (for example the category of all
complexes with components in a given Quillen exact category). By contrast, we aim at axiomatising
a class of not necessarily pretriangulated dg categories endowed with additional
structure.

Definition 4.3
.

Let
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})
and
(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{S}^{\prime})
be exact dg categories.
A morphism
F:\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
in
\mathrm{Hqe}
is exact if the induced functor
\mathcal{H}_{3t}(\mathcal{A})\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{3t}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
sends objects in
\mathcal{S}
to objects in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.
We denote by
\mathrm{Hqe}_{ex}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
the subset of
\mathrm{Hqe}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
consisiting of exact morphisms.

Remark 4.4
.

Note that the class
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{3t}(\mathcal{A})
is stable under isomorphisms.
Let
\overline{\imath}
be a morphism in
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
.
If a representative
i
of
\overline{\imath}
is an inflation, then any representative in

\overline{\imath}
is also an inflation.
Thus, being an inflation is a property of the morphism
\overline{\imath}
.
We have an even stronger property: If we view
\overline{\imath}:A_{0}\rightarrow A_{1}
as an
object in
\mathrm{Mor}(H^{0}(\mathcal{A}))
, then being an inflation is stable under isomorphisms in
\mathrm{Mor}(H^{0}(\mathcal{A}))
, cf. Lemma
3.27
.

In the setting of Axiom
{\mathrm{Ex}}2
, since the homotopy pullback is unique up to a unique isomorphism
in
\mathcal{H}_{3t}(\mathcal{A})
, by Lemma
2.11
, the object
{j^{\prime}}
is unique up to a canonical isomorphism
in
H^{0}(\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{A}))
.

Remark 4.5
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
be additive dg categories.

a)

By Lemma
3.7
and its dual, the exact dg structures on
\mathcal{A}
are in bijection with the exact structures on
\tau_{\leq 0}\mathcal{A}
.

b)

Let
F:\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
be a quasi-equivalence of dg categories.
Then
F
induces an equivalence of categories
\mathcal{H}_{3t}(\mathcal{A})\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathcal{H}_{3t}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
which
preserves and reflects the property of being homotopy short exact.
Thus, the quasi-equivalence
F
induces a bijection between the class of exact dg structures
on
\mathcal{A}
and that on
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
.

For two objects
C
and
A
in
\mathcal{A}
, consider the set of ‘equivalence classes of extensions’

\mathbb{E}(C,A)
(resp.
\mathbb{E}^{\prime}(FC,FA)
), to be defined in Definition
4.14
, which is associated with the exact structure on
\mathcal{A}
(resp.
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
).
The quasi-equivalence
F
induces a bijection between
\mathbb{E}(C,A)
and
\mathbb{E}^{\prime}(C,A)
, cf. Lemma
4.15
.

c)

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an exact dg category and
\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathrm{pretr}(\mathcal{A})
be the inclusion of
\mathcal{A}
into its pretriangulated hull.
Let
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
be the additive closure of
\mathcal{A}
in
\mathrm{pretr}(\mathcal{A})
.
Since
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
is an additive category, the inclusion of
\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is a quasi-equivalence.
By b), we may replace
\mathcal{A}
by
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
and assume that the dg category
\mathcal{A}
is such that
Z^{0}(\mathcal{A})
is an additive category.

Note that an object
f:A\rightarrow B
in
H^{0}(\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{A}))
is a deflation (resp. an inflation) if and only if it admits a homotopy kernel (resp. homotopy cokernel) (cf. Definition
3.26
) which is a conflation. Observe that homotopy equivalences are both inflations and deflations by axioms
Ex0, Ex2 and Ex
2^{op}
.

Example 4.6
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an additive category which we consider as a dg category concentrated in degree 0.

Let
A
,
B
and
C
be objects in
\mathcal{A}
and consider a 3-term h-complex
X
in
\mathcal{A}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

Since
\mathcal{A}
is concentrated in degree zero, the homotopy
h
is zero.
By Example
3.5
, the 3-term h-complex
X
is a homotopy short exact sequence if and only if the corresponding sequence
0\rightarrow A\xrightarrow{f}B\xrightarrow{j}C\rightarrow 0
is a kernel-cokernel pair in the additive category
\mathcal{A}
.
Also, two kernel-cokernel pairs are isomorphic (resp. equivalent) if and only if the corresponding homotopy short exact sequences are isomorphic (resp. equivalent).

By
[
20
, Appendix A]
, the axioms of a Quillen exact structure on
\mathcal{A}
correspond to the axioms of an exact dg structure on
\mathcal{A}
.
Therefore, to endow an additive category
\mathcal{A}
with a Quillen exact structure is the same as to endow
\mathcal{A}
with an exact dg structure.

Example 4.7
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a pretriangulated dg category.
By Remark
4.5
, we may assume that the dg category
\mathcal{A}
is strictly pretriangulated.
Then each morphism
f:A\rightarrow B
admits a homotopy cokernel

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Cone}(f).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

Dually, each morphism admits a homotopy kernel.
Also, a
3
-term homotopy complex in
\mathcal{A}
is homotopy left exact if and only if it is homotopy right exact.
It is not hard to check that the class of all homotopy short exact sequences in
\mathcal{A}
defines an exact structure on
\mathcal{A}
.
If not stated otherwise, we always consider this maximal exact structure on a pretriangulated dg category.

Example-Definition 4.8
.

A full dg subcategory
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
of an exact dg category
\mathcal{A}
is extension-closed provided that for each conflation in
\mathcal{A}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

if
A
and
C
belong to
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
, then so does
B
.

Let
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
be an extension-closed subcategory of an exact dg category
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})
.
Let
X
be a conflation in
\mathcal{A}
with all terms in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
.
Then
X
can be seen as a 3-term h-complex in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
.
By Remark
3.2
, the object
X
is a homotopy short exact sequence in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
.

We have an inclusion functor
\mathcal{H}_{3t}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}_{3t}(\mathcal{A})
.
Let
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
be the class of objects in
\mathcal{H}_{3t}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
whose image in
\mathcal{H}_{3t}(\mathcal{A})
belongs to
\mathcal{S}
.
We show that the class
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
defines an exact dg structure on
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
.
Axiom
{\mathrm{Ex}}0
is straightforward to check.
Axiom
{\mathrm{Ex}}1
follows from Lemma
3.37
and the fact that
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is extension-closed in
\mathcal{A}
.
Consider the span
S
of a morphism in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
along an inflation in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
.
It admits a homotopy pushout in
\mathcal{A}
, and by Proposition
3.34
,
the homotopy pushout
X
is an extension of objects in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
and hence remains an object in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
.
Again by Proposition
3.34
, the pushout of the inflation in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is an inflation in
\mathcal{A}
and
the conflation that this inflation belongs to is in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.
Thus Axiom
\mathrm{Ex}2^{op}
is satisfied.
Dually, one checks Axiom
\mathrm{Ex}2
.

Proposition 4.9
.

Suppose
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})
is an exact dg category.
The following statements hold:

a)

The diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\oplus B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[}1\ 0{]}^{\intercal}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[}0\ 1{]}}

is a conflation for
A,B\in\mathcal{A}
.

b)

If a morphism
g:B\rightarrow C
in
Z^{0}(\mathcal{A})
admits a homotopy kernel and for some
h:B^{\prime}\rightarrow B
the composition
gh:B^{\prime}\rightarrow C
is a deflation, then
g
is a deflation.

b)
{}^{\text{op}}

If a morphism
f:A\rightarrow B
admits a homotopy cokernel and for some
e:B\rightarrow B^{\prime}
the composition
ef:A\rightarrow B^{\prime}
is an inflation, then
f
is an inflation.

c)

The direct sum of two conflations is a conflation.

d)

Axiom
{\mathrm{Ex}1}^{op}
holds.

Proof.

Suppose we are in the situation of Axiom Ex2.

Claim 1:

B\oplus C^{\prime}\xrightarrow{[j,c]}C
is a deflation.

We have a conflation
X\in\mathcal{H}_{3t}(\mathcal{A})
of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\mathrlap{.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

By Axiom Ex2, the cospan
B\xrightarrow{j}C\xleftarrow{c}C^{\prime}

admits a homotopy pullback
Y
:

, we have a morphism

(4.9.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}

,

is homotopy bicartesian.

Then
X^{\prime\prime}
is a homotopy short exact sequence.

Claim 2:

X^{\prime\prime}
is a conflation.

Consider the conflation
X
and the object
f^{\prime}:A\rightarrow B^{\prime}
in
H^{0}(\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{A}))
.
The span
S:B^{\prime}\xleftarrow{f^{\prime}}A\xrightarrow{f}B

admits a homotopy pushout
Z

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\prime}\oplus B}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{1}=\begin{bmatrix}0\\
\mathrm{id}\end{bmatrix}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime\prime}=\begin{bmatrix}j^{\prime}\\
b\end{bmatrix}}

where
s_{3}=\begin{bmatrix}-h^{\prime}\\
s_{1}\end{bmatrix}
.
By Axiom Ex
2^{op}
, the morphism
B^{\prime}\xrightarrow{[b,j^{\prime}]^{\intercal}}B\oplus C^{\prime}
is an inflation.
So by Proposition
3.34
, there exists a morphism
X\rightarrow\widetilde{X^{\prime\prime}}
in
\mathcal{H}_{3t}(\mathcal{A})

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\prime}\oplus B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime\prime}}

where
\widetilde{X^{\prime\prime}}
is the 3-term h-complex in the second row.
By the uniqueness of homotopy cokernels,
the homotopy right exact sequence
\widetilde{X^{\prime\prime}}
is isomorphic to
X^{\prime\prime}
.
By Axiom Ex
2^{op}
,
\widetilde{X^{\prime\prime}}
is a conflation.
This proves Claim 2.
Then the morphism
B\oplus C^{\prime}\xrightarrow{[j,c]}C
is a deflation.
This proves Claim 1.
Note that during the proof we only use Axioms Ex2 and Ex
2^{op}
,
so the dual of the claims also holds.

Now we are ready to prove
\mathrm{b})
.
Suppose we have a homotopy left exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

From the cospan

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces gh}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
,
we know that the morphism
B\oplus B^{\prime}\xrightarrow{[g,gh]}C
is a deflation by claim 1.
Then the morphism

{[}g\ 0{]}:B\oplus B^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\begin{bmatrix}1&-h\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}}B\oplus B^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\begin{bmatrix}g,gh\end{bmatrix}}C

is also a deflation by Axiom Ex1.
The 3-term h-complex

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K\oplus B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\oplus B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\mathrlap{,}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[}h{,}\;0{]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}

where
u=\begin{bmatrix}f&0\\
0&\mathrm{id}_{B^{\prime}}\end{bmatrix}
and
v={[}g{,}\;0{]}
, is homotopy left exact, as the direct sum of a homotopy left exact sequence and a trivial 3-term h-complex.
Therefore it is a conflation.
We have the following morphism of homotopy left exact sequences

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K\oplus B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\oplus B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[}1{,}0{]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[}h{,}\;0{]}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[}1{,}0{]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

where the left square is homotopy bicartesian, we infer that the morphism
g:B\rightarrow C
is a deflation by Ex
2^{op}
. This proves Statement b).

Since the composition

\mathrm{id}_{B}:B\xrightarrow{\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}}A\oplus B\xrightarrow{[0,\;1]}B

is a deflation and the morphism
A\oplus B\xrightarrow{[0,\;1]}B
admits a homotopy kernel,
it is a deflation by Statement b).
So Statement a) follows.

Observe that the morphism

is a homotopy pullback of the deflation

along

Similarly, the morphism

is also a deflation.

is a deflation.

{\mathrm{Ex}1}^{op}
and then one proves b)
op
dually.

be an inflation with the homotopy cokernel given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

{\mathrm{Ex}2}^{op}
, the span

admits a homotopy pushout

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{\prime\prime}}}

By Axiom
{\mathrm{Ex}1}
, the morphism
[{j^{\prime}},{f^{\prime\prime}}]\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&{j}\end{bmatrix}
is a deflation.
By the above diagram, the morphism
{j^{\prime}}[1,{f^{\prime}}]
is homotopic to it and hence is also a deflation.
So by Statement b), the morphism
{j^{\prime}}
is a deflation.
Hence the morphism
f^{\prime}f
is an inflation.
This proves Statement d).
∎

The following diagram lemma is useful.
It is a direct consequence of Lemma
3.36
and Axiom
\mathrm{Ex}2
(or
\mathrm{Ex}2^{op}
).

Let
\alpha:X\rightarrow X^{\prime}
be a morphism in
\mathcal{H}_{3t}(\mathcal{A})
.
Denote by
\overline{a}:A\rightarrow A^{\prime}
(resp.
\overline{c}:C\rightarrow C^{\prime}
) the restriction of
\alpha
to
A
(resp.
C
).
Then
\alpha
can be written as a composition
X\xrightarrow{\beta}\widetilde{X}\xrightarrow{\gamma}X^{\prime}
, where
\widetilde{X}
is a conflation with ends
A^{\prime}
and
C
, and the morphism
\beta
restricts to
\overline{a}:A\rightarrow A^{\prime}
and
\mathrm{id}_{C}
, and the morphism
\gamma
restricts to
\mathrm{id}_{A^{\prime}}
and
\overline{c}:C\rightarrow C^{\prime}
.

Remark 4.11
.

If we use the biadditive bifunctor
\mathbb{E}
which is to be defined in
4.2
, then we have
[X^{\prime}]\in\mathbb{E}(C,A^{\prime})
and the morphism
\beta
(resp.
\gamma
) shows that
[\widetilde{X}]=\overline{a}_{*}[X]
(resp.
[\widetilde{X}]=\overline{c}^{*}[X^{\prime}]
).

Then the diagram (
4.12.2
)

(4.12.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\prime}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}}

is a homotopy pullback square, and hence the corresponding 3-term homotopy complex is a conflation, by Claim 1 in the proof of Proposition
4.9
.

Proof.

The same proof for Claim 2 in Proposition
4.9
applies here as well.
Note that in that proof, we only use the homotopy right exactness of
3
-term homotopy complex (
4.9.2
).
∎

Combining Lemma
4.10
, Lemma
4.12
, and Lemma
3.38
, we have

Corollary 4.13
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an exact dg category.
If a morphism
\alpha:X\rightarrow X^{\prime}
of conflations restricts to homotopy equivalences on both ends, it restricts to a homotopy equivalence between the middle terms.

.

A morphism
\theta:X_{1}\rightarrow X_{2}
in
\mathcal{H}_{3t}(\mathcal{A})
is called an equivalence if it restricts to
\mathrm{id}_{A}
and
\mathrm{id}_{C}
.
Note that by Corollary
4.13
, an equivalence of conflations is necessarily an isomorphism in
\mathcal{H}_{3t}(\mathcal{A})
.
For a conflation, we denote by
[X]
the equivalence class to which
X
belongs.

We define
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{S}}(C,A)
to be the set of equivalence classes of conflations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

with fixed ends
A
and
C
.
When there is no risk of confusion, we will simply denote it by
\mathbb{E}(C,A)
.

Lemma 4.15
.

Let
F:\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
be a quasi-equivalence of dg categories.
Suppose
\mathcal{A}
is an exact dg category.
Equip
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
with the induced exact structure from the quasi-equivalence
F
.
For two objects
C
,
A
in
\mathcal{A}
, consider the sets
\mathbb{E}(C,A)
(resp.
\mathbb{E}^{\prime}(FC,FA)
), which is associated with the exact structure on
\mathcal{A}
(resp.
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
).
The quasi-equivalence
F
induces a bijection

\mathbb{E}(C,A)\rightarrow\mathbb{E}^{\prime}(FC,FA).

Proof.

It is straightforward to verify that the above map is an injection.
We now demonstrate that it is a surjection.
For an object
X\in\mathcal{H}_{3t}(\mathcal{A})
, we denote by
FX
the object in
\mathcal{H}_{3t}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
induced by
F
.

Let
[X^{\prime}]
be an element in
\mathbb{E}^{\prime}(FC,FA)
.
Suppose
X^{\prime}
is of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FC\mathrlap{.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}}

Since
F:\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is a quasi-equivalence, we have a homotopy equivalence
FB\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}B^{\prime}
for some
B\in\mathcal{A}
.
Hence we have an isomorphism in
H^{0}(\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}))
of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FB}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FC}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FC\mathrlap{.}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F{(}j{)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}}

By Lemma
3.27
, we may assume
B^{\prime}=FB
and
j^{\prime}=F(j)
for some
j:B\rightarrow C
in
Z^{0}(\mathcal{A})
.
The morphism
j:B\rightarrow C
is a deflation.
Suppose its homotopy kernel
Y
is of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\mathrlap{.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

Then we have an isomorphism
\theta:X^{\prime}\rightarrow FY
in
\mathcal{H}_{3t}(\mathcal{A})
which restricts to the identity of
j^{\prime}:FB\rightarrow FC
in
H^{0}(\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}))
.
Let
\overline{a^{\prime}}:FA^{\prime}\rightarrow FA
be the restriction of
\theta
to
FA^{\prime}
.
Then we have a morphism
a:A\rightarrow A^{\prime}
such that
F(a)
is a homotopy inverse of
a^{\prime}
.
We have an isomorphism in
H^{0}(\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}))
of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f=\widetilde{f}a}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{f}}

By Lemma
3.27
, we deduce that
X^{\prime}
is equivalent to
FX
where
X
is of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\mathrlap{.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

∎

and a map

.
Below we will prove that there exists a unique equivalence class of conflations

,

of

,

of

with a morphism
\theta:X\rightarrow X^{\prime}

which restricts to the identity on

.

{\mathrm{Ex}2}^{op}

, we may assume that the restriction of

,

and that
S_{1}
is a homotopy pushout of the cospan
L:A^{\prime}\xleftarrow{a}A\xrightarrow{f}B
,
where
a
is a morphism in
Z^{0}(\mathcal{A})
representing the morphism
\overline{a}
. The homotopy square
S_{2}
restricts to a cospan
L^{\prime}
and there exists a (non-unique) isomorphism
L\rightarrow L^{\prime}
that restricts to the identity of
f
.
By the universal property of homotopy pushouts (cf. Remark
3.11
), there is a unique morphism
\mu
in
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Sq}}(\mathcal{A})
from
S_{1}
to
S_{2}
that restricts to the isomorphism
L\rightarrow L^{\prime}
.
We continue to denote by
S_{i}
the morphism in
H^{0}(\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{A}))
from
f
to
f_{i}
given by the square
S_{i}
for
i=1
,
2
.
According to Lemma
3.22
, the morphism
\mu:S_{1}\rightarrow S_{2}
yields morphisms
\mathrm{id}_{f}
,
S_{1}
,
S_{2}
, and a morphism
\alpha
from
f_{1}:A^{\prime}\rightarrow B_{1}
to
f_{2}:A^{\prime}\rightarrow B_{2}
that restricts to
\mathrm{id}_{A^{\prime}}
and satisfies
\alpha\circ S_{1}=S_{2}\circ\mathrm{id}_{f}
in
H^{0}(\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{A}))
.
By the universal property of homotopy pushouts, the morphism
\alpha
induces a morphism
\theta_{3}:X_{1}\rightarrow X_{2}
in
\mathcal{H}_{3t}(\mathcal{A})
such that
\theta_{3}\circ\theta_{1}=\theta_{2}
.
Then, the morphism
\theta_{3}
is an equivalence between
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
.
Since
\theta_{3}
is necessarily an isomorphism by Corollary
4.13
, we conclude that the morphism
\alpha:S_{1}\rightarrow S_{2}
is an isomorphism.

Proposition 4.16
.

The operation
\overline{a}:A\rightarrow A^{\prime}\mapsto\overline{a}_{*}:\mathbb{E}(C,A)\rightarrow\mathbb{E}(C,A^{\prime})
,
where an element
[X]
is sent to
\overline{a}_{*}[X]{\coloneqq}[X^{\prime}]
which is specified by the above mentioned property,
together with its dual operation
\overline{c}:C^{\prime}\rightarrow C\mapsto\overline{c}^{*}:\mathbb{E}(C,A)\rightarrow\mathbb{E}(C^{\prime},A)
,
makes
\mathbb{E}:H^{0}(\mathcal{A})^{op}\times H^{0}(\mathcal{A})\rightarrow\mathrm{Set}
into a bifunctor.

Remark 4.17
.

In Subsection
4.3
, we will prove that
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
carries a canonical extriangulated structure in the
sense of Nakaoka–Palu
[
35
]
whose extension bifunctor is
\mathbb{E}
.

Proof.

Suppose we are given
[X]\in\mathbb{E}(C,A)
,
\overline{a}:A\rightarrow A^{\prime}
and
\overline{c}:C^{\prime}\rightarrow C
, where
X
is given by the diagram (
4.15.1
).

Put
\overline{a}_{*}[X]=[X_{1}]
,
\overline{c}^{*}[X_{1}]=[X_{2}]
and
\overline{c}^{*}[X]=[X_{3}]
.
Then the conflations
X_{1}
,
X_{2}
and
X_{3}
take the following form, respectively

By the definition of
[X_{2}]
, we have a homotopy cartesian square which gives rise to a morphism
\alpha
in
H^{0}(\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{A}))
from
j_{2}:E_{2}\rightarrow C^{\prime}
to
j_{1}:E_{1}\rightarrow C
.
We also have a morphism
\beta
from
j_{3}:E_{3}\rightarrow C^{\prime}
to
j_{1}:E_{1}\rightarrow C^{\prime}
.
By Lemma
3.36
, we have a morphism
\gamma
from
j_{3}:E_{3}\rightarrow C^{\prime}
to
j_{2}:E_{2}\rightarrow C^{\prime}
which restricts to
\mathrm{id}_{C^{\prime}}
and such that
\alpha\circ\gamma=\beta
.
The associated diagram in
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
is as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f_{3}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\jmath_{3}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\jmath}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\jmath_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\jmath_{1}}}

By universal property of the homotopy pullbacks (cf. Remark
3.11
), the morphism
\gamma
induces a morphism from
X_{3}
to
X_{2}
.
It clearly restricts to
\overline{a}:A\rightarrow A^{\prime}
.
Therefore
\overline{a}_{*}[X_{3}]=[X_{2}]
.
∎

Our next goal is to demonstrate that
\mathbb{E}(C,A)
forms an abelian group for every pair
(C,A)
of objects in
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
, ensuring that
\mathbb{E}:H^{0}(\mathcal{A})^{op}\times H^{0}(\mathcal{A})\rightarrow\mathrm{Ab}
is a biadditive bifunctor.

We begin by describing the addition operation on
\mathbb{E}(C,A)
.
Let
[X]
and
[X^{\prime}]
be two elements in
\mathbb{E}(C,A)
given by the diagram (
4.14.1
).
Put
\overline{a}=[1\ 1]:A\oplus A\rightarrow A
and
\overline{c}=[1\ 1]^{\intercal}:C\rightarrow C\oplus C
. Recall from Proposition
c)
that the direct sum
X\oplus X^{\prime}
is also a conflation.
Then the sum
[X]+[X^{\prime}]
is defined to be
\overline{c}^{*}\overline{a}_{*}[X\oplus X^{\prime}]
.

Lemma 4.18
.

The above addition operation is well-defined, associative and commutative and that the equivalence class of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\oplus C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[}0{,}1{]}}

is a zero element.

Proof.

It is straightforward to check the well-definedness and commutativity.
To show that the above conflation is a zero element, observe that we have the following morphism in
\mathcal{H}_{3t}(\mathcal{A})

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\oplus A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\oplus C\oplus B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\oplus C}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[}0{,}1{]^{\intercal}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[}0{,}\;j{,}\;1{]^{\intercal}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[}1{,}\;1{]^{\intercal}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}}

where
f^{\prime}=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&0\\
0&f\end{bmatrix}
,
j^{\prime}=\begin{bmatrix}0&1&0\\
0&0&j\end{bmatrix}
,
h^{\prime}=\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
0&h\end{bmatrix}
,
s=[0,\;h,\;0]^{\intercal}
.

To show the associativity, observe that we have the equality

\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\circ\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
1&0\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\circ\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}:A\rightarrow A\oplus A\oplus A.

∎

Conflations in the equivalence class of zero elements are called
splitting
.

The following statements are equivalent:

1)

is a splitting conflation.

\overline{f}
is a split monomorphism in

.

is a split epimorphism in
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
.

Proof.

The implication
1)\Rightarrow 2)
follows from Corollary
4.13
.

We show
2)\Rightarrow 1)
.

Suppose
\overline{f}
is a split monomorphism in
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
.
Then we have an isomorphism from
A\rightarrow A\oplus C^{\prime}
to
f:A\rightarrow B
in
H^{0}(\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{A}))
, which restricts to
\mathrm{id}_{A}
.
So their homotopy cokernels are isomorphic and hence
C^{\prime}
is homotopy equivalent to
C
.
So we have an isomorphism in
H^{0}(\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{A}))
from

to

which restricts to
\mathrm{id}_{A}
.
Then it induces an isomorphism
\alpha
in

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}

is homotopic to

.

be a homotopy inverse of

.

gives rise to a morphism

Then
[-X]
, where
-X
is of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

is an inverse of
[X]
under the addition defined above. So this addition makes
\mathbb{E}(C,A)
into an abelian group.

Proof.

It is straightforward to check that the element
[-X]
is well-defined.
Consider the following diagram in
\mathcal{A}

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-39.56033pt}{-24.72476pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{

{{{{{}}{

{}{}}}{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{31.9317pt}{-24.72476pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

where the diagonal morphisms are zero and where
f^{\prime}=\begin{bmatrix}-f&0\\
0&f\end{bmatrix}
,
j^{\prime}=\begin{bmatrix}j&0\\
0&j\end{bmatrix}
and
h^{\prime}=\begin{bmatrix}-h&0\\
0&h\end{bmatrix}
. This diagram gives rise to a morphism
\alpha:X\rightarrow-X\oplus X
in
\mathcal{H}_{3t}(\mathcal{A})
.
Next, we apply Lemma
4.10
to the morphism
\alpha
.
Put
a=[1,1]:A\oplus A\rightarrow A
,
a^{\prime}=[-1,1]^{\intercal}:A\rightarrow A\oplus A
and
c=[1,1]^{\intercal}:C\rightarrow C\oplus C
.
Then we have
\alpha=\gamma\circ\beta
where
\beta:X\rightarrow\widetilde{X}
,
\gamma:\widetilde{X}\rightarrow-X\oplus X
, and
[\widetilde{X}]=\overline{a^{\prime}}_{*}[X]=\overline{c}^{*}[-X\oplus X]
.
So we have

[X]+[-X]=\overline{a}_{*}\overline{c}^{*}[-X\oplus X]=\overline{a}_{*}[\widetilde{X}]=\overline{a}_{*}\overline{a^{\prime}}_{*}[X]=0_{*}[X]=0.

This shows that
[-X]
is an additive inverse of
[X]
.

∎

Proposition 4.22
.

The bifunctor
\mathbb{E}
is a biadditive bifunctor from
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})^{op}\times H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
to the category
\mathrm{Ab}
of abelian groups.

Proof.

Let
X_{i}
,
i=1
,
2
, be a conflation of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{i}}

and
\overline{b}:A\rightarrow A^{\prime}
a morphism in
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
.
Put
a=[1,1]:A\oplus A\rightarrow A
,
a^{\prime}=[1,1]:A^{\prime}\oplus A^{\prime}\rightarrow A^{\prime}
,
c=[1,1]^{\intercal}:C\rightarrow C\oplus C
and
d=\begin{bmatrix}b&0\\
0&b\end{bmatrix}:A\oplus A\rightarrow A^{\prime}\oplus A^{\prime}
.
We show that
\overline{b}_{*}:\mathbb{E}(C,A)\rightarrow\mathbb{E}(C,A^{\prime})
is a morphism of abelian groups.
Put
[\widetilde{X_{i}}]=\overline{b}_{*}[X_{i}]
,
i=1,2
.
Then, by definition, we have
d_{*}[X_{1}\oplus X_{2}]=[\widetilde{X_{1}}\oplus\widetilde{X_{2}}]
.
So we have

\displaystyle\overline{b}_{*}([X_{1}]+[X_{2}])

\displaystyle=\overline{b}_{*}\overline{a}_{*}\overline{c}^{*}[X_{1}\oplus X_{2}]=\overline{a}^{\prime}_{*}\overline{d}_{*}\overline{c}^{*}[X_{1}\oplus X_{2}]

\displaystyle=\overline{a}^{\prime}_{*}\overline{c}^{*}\overline{d}_{*}[X_{1}\oplus X_{2}]=\overline{a}^{\prime}_{*}\overline{c}^{*}[\widetilde{X_{1}}\oplus\widetilde{X_{2}}]

\displaystyle=\widetilde{X_{1}}+\widetilde{X_{2}}=\overline{b}_{*}[X_{1}]+\overline{b}_{*}[X_{2}].

So the map
\overline{b}_{*}
is a morphism of abelian groups.

X
be a conflation of the form

Suppose we have morphisms
\overline{b}_{i}:A\rightarrow A^{\prime}
in
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
for
i=1
,
2
. We show that
(\overline{b}_{1}+\overline{b}_{2})_{*}=(\overline{b}_{1})_{*}+(\overline{b}_{2})_{*}
.
Put
d^{\prime}=\begin{bmatrix}b_{1}&0\\
0&b_{2}\end{bmatrix}
and
a^{\prime\prime}=[1,1]^{\intercal}:A\rightarrow A\oplus A
.
Put
[Y_{i}]=(\overline{b}_{i})_{*}[X]
for
i=1
,
2
.
We have a canonical morphism
\alpha:X\rightarrow X\oplus X
which is componentwise given by the morphism
[1,1]^{\intercal}
.
By Lemma
4.10
, the morphism
\alpha
factorizes as
\alpha=\gamma\circ\beta
where we have
\beta:X\rightarrow\widetilde{X}
,
\gamma:\widetilde{X}\rightarrow X\oplus X
and
[\widetilde{X}]=\overline{a}^{\prime\prime}_{*}[X]=\overline{c}^{*}[X\oplus X]
.
Then we have

\displaystyle(\overline{b}_{1}+\overline{b}_{2})_{*}[X]

\displaystyle=\overline{a}^{\prime}_{*}\overline{d}^{\prime}_{*}\overline{a}^{\prime\prime}_{*}[X]=\overline{a}^{\prime}_{*}\overline{d}^{\prime}_{*}\overline{c}^{*}[X\oplus X]

\displaystyle=\overline{a}^{\prime}_{*}\overline{c}^{*}\overline{d}^{\prime}_{*}[X\oplus X]=\overline{a}^{\prime}_{*}\overline{c}^{*}[Y_{1}\oplus Y_{2}]

\displaystyle=Y_{1}+Y_{2}=(\overline{b}_{1})_{*}[X]+(\overline{b}_{2})_{*}[X].

Thus we have

(\overline{b}_{1}+\overline{b}_{2})_{*}=(\overline{b}_{1})_{*}+(\overline{b}_{2})_{*}
.
This, together with its dual, shows that the bifunctor
\mathbb{E}
is biadditive.
∎

For two elements
\delta_{i}=[X_{i}]\in\mathbb{E}(C_{i},A_{i})
,
i=1
,
2
, let
\delta_{1}\oplus\delta_{2}
be the element in

\mathbb{E}(C_{1}\oplus C_{2},A_{1}\oplus A_{2})\simeq\mathbb{E}(C_{1},A_{1})\oplus\mathbb{E}(C_{2},A_{2})\oplus\mathbb{E}(C_{1},A_{2})\oplus\mathbb{E}(C_{2},A_{1})

corresponding to the element
(\delta_{1},\delta_{2},0,0)
.

.

is the equivalence class of

.

for

and

.

.

where

.

, we have

.

4.3.
The canonical extriangulated structure on
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})

In this subsection, we show that
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
carries a canonical extriangulated structure, as announced
in Remark
4.17
.

We define the realisation
\mathfrak{s}
of the bifunctor
\mathbb{E}
as follows: for an element
\delta=[X]\in\mathbb{E}(C,A)
, where
X
is as follows

\hbox to127.28pt{\vbox to40.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 63.64122pt\lower-30.28264pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

4.13
.

Lemma 4.24
.

The map
\mathfrak{s}
defined above is an additive realization of
\mathbb{E}
.

Proof.

We first show that
\mathfrak{s}
is a realization.
Suppose we have
\delta=[X]\in\mathbb{E}(C,A)
,
\delta^{\prime}=[X^{\prime}]\in\mathbb{E}(C^{\prime},A^{\prime})
,
\overline{a}:A\rightarrow A^{\prime}
and
\overline{c}:C\rightarrow C^{\prime}
such that
\overline{a}_{*}[X]=\overline{c}^{*}[X^{\prime}]=[X^{\prime\prime}]\in\mathbb{E}(C,A^{\prime})
. Put
\mathfrak{s}(\delta)=[A\xrightarrow{\overline{f}}B\xrightarrow{\overline{\jmath}}C]
and
\mathfrak{s}(\delta^{\prime})=[A^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\overline{f^{\prime}}}B^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\overline{\jmath^{\prime}}}C^{\prime}]
.
We have a composition of morphism of conflations
X\rightarrow X^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow X^{\prime}
which restricts to
\overline{a}:A\rightarrow A^{\prime}
and
\overline{c}:C\rightarrow C^{\prime}
.
This morphism then gives a morphism
\overline{b}:B\rightarrow B^{\prime}
which makes the following diagram in
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
commutative

\hbox to135.7pt{\vbox to53.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 67.84955pt\lower-27.51862pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

We now show that
\mathfrak{s}
is additive.
By definition, for
0\in\mathbb{E}(C,A)
, we have

\mathfrak{s}(0)=[A\xrightarrow{\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}}A\oplus C\xrightarrow{[0{,}1]}C].

By Lemma
4.23
, we have
\mathfrak{s}(\delta\oplus\delta^{\prime})=\mathfrak{s}(\delta)\oplus\mathfrak{s}(\delta^{\prime})
.
∎

Remark 4.25
.

By Remark
4.5
, the exact dg structures on
\mathcal{A}
are the same as
the exact dg structures on
\tau_{\leq 0}\mathcal{A}
.
Note that we have
H^{0}(\tau_{\leq 0}\mathcal{A})=H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
.
By definition, the triple
(H^{0}(\mathcal{A}),\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
remains unchanged when we replace
\mathcal{A}
by
\tau_{\leq 0}\mathcal{A}
.

Theorem 4.26
.

The triple
(H^{0}(\mathcal{A}),\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
forms an extriangulated category.

Proof.

(\mathrm{ET3})
Let
\delta=[X]\in\mathbb{E}(C,A)
,

.

and

.

the left hand square can be lifted (in a non-unique way) to a morphism from
f:A\rightarrow B
to
f^{\prime}:A^{\prime}\rightarrow B^{\prime}
in
H^{0}(\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{A}))
.
It then induces a morphism
X\rightarrow X^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{H}_{3t}(\mathcal{A})
.
The restriction gives a morphism
c:C\rightarrow C^{\prime}
in
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
.
By Lemma
4.10
, we have
a_{*}\delta=c^{*}\delta^{\prime}
.
This proves (ET3).
Axiom
(ET3)
op
is proved dually.

(\mathrm{ET4})
Consider
\delta=[X]\in\mathbb{E}(D,A)
and
\delta^{\prime}=[X^{\prime}]\in\mathbb{E}(F,B)
which are realized respectively by
A\xrightarrow{\overline{f}}B\xrightarrow{\overline{\jmath}}D
and
B\xrightarrow{\overline{f^{\prime}}}C\xrightarrow{\overline{\jmath^{\prime}}}F
.

Since a composition of inflations is an inflation, the object
f^{\prime}f:A\rightarrow C
is an inflation and there exists a conflation
X^{\prime\prime}
such that
[X^{\prime\prime}]
is realized by
A\xrightarrow{\overline{f^{\prime}f}}C\xrightarrow{\overline{h}}E
.
The commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f^{\prime}f}}

in
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
can be lifted to a morphism in
H^{0}(\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{A}))
from
f:A\rightarrow B
to
f^{\prime}f:A\rightarrow C
.
This morphism induces a morphism from
X
to
X^{\prime\prime}
which restricts to a homotopy bicartesian square
Y_{1}
whose image in
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathrm{Sq},\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}))
is isomorphic to

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;.

Let
\overline{\jmath}_{*}[X^{\prime}]=[X^{\prime\prime\prime}]\in\mathbb{E}(F,B)
.
We have a morphism
X^{\prime}\rightarrow X^{\prime\prime\prime}
which, by restriction to
f^{\prime}:B\rightarrow C
, yields a homotopy bicartesian square

.
Both

and

are homotopy pushouts of

So there exists an isomorphism
Y_{1}\rightarrow Y_{2}
in
\mathcal{D}(\mathrm{Sq})
which is compatible with the isomorphisms
R(Y_{i})\rightarrow S
where
R:\mathcal{D}(\mathrm{Sq})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(\mathrm{Sp})
is the restriction functor.
So
[X^{\prime\prime\prime}]
can be realized by
D\xrightarrow{\overline{d}}E\xrightarrow{\overline{e}}F
such that
\overline{\jmath^{\prime}}=\overline{e}\overline{h}
. This shows (ET4). Axiom
(
ET4
op
) can be shown dually.
∎

Definition 4.27
.

An extriangulated category
\mathcal{C}
is algebraic, if it is equivalent, as an extriangulated category, to
(H^{0}(\mathcal{A}),\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
for an exact dg category
\mathcal{A}
.

By Remark
4.25
, the underlying dg category of an exact dg category, which enhances an extriangulated category, could always be taken to be connective.

4.4.
Subcategories, closed subbifunctors and exact dg substructures

In this subsection, for an exact dg category
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})
, we demonstrate the existence of a canonical bijection between the lattice of exact substructures of
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})
and the lattice of closed subbifunctors of
\mathbb{E}
, where
(H^{0}(\mathcal{A}),\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
is the associated extriangulated category of
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})
, cf. Theorem
4.33
.
By leveraging Ogawa’s notion of defect
[
36
, Definition 2.4]
and Iyama–Nakaoka–Palu’s notion of almost split extension
[
18
, Definition 2.1]
, for certain class of exact dg categories, we establish a bijection between the class of exact substructures and the Boolean lattice of subsets of a certain set of objects, cf. Corollary
4.39
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
be an extriangulated category.

Definition 4.28

(
[
16
]
, Lemma 3.15)
.

Let
\mathbb{F}\subseteq\mathbb{E}
be an additive subbifunctor. The following are equivalent.

(1)

\mathbb{F}
is closed on the right, i.e. for any
\mathfrak{s}|_{\mathbb{F}}
-conflation
A\xrightarrow{f}B\xrightarrow{j}C
, the sequence

\mathbb{F}(-,C)\xRightarrow{f_{*}}\mathbb{F}(-,B)\xRightarrow{j_{*}}\mathbb{F}(-,A)

is exact.

(2)

\mathbb{F}
is closed on the left, i.e. for any
\mathfrak{s}|_{\mathbb{F}}
-conflation
A\xrightarrow{f}B\xrightarrow{j}C
, the sequence

\mathbb{F}(A,-)\xRightarrow{j^{*}}\mathbb{F}(B,-)\xRightarrow{f^{*}}\mathbb{F}(C,-)

is exact.

Thus, we simply say
\mathbb{F}\subseteq\mathbb{E}
is closed, if either of the conditions is satisfied.

Proposition 4.29

(
[
16
]
, Proposition 3.16)
.

For any additive subbifunctor
\mathbb{F}\subseteq\mathbb{E}
, the following are equivalent.

(1)

(\mathcal{C},\mathbb{F},\mathfrak{s}|_{\mathbb{F}})
is extriangulated.

(2)

\mathfrak{s}|_{\mathbb{F}}
-inflations are closed under composition.

(3)

\mathfrak{s}|_{\mathbb{F}}
-deflations are closed under composition.

(4)

\mathbb{F}
is closed.

Definition 4.30

(
[
36
]
, Definition 2.4)
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
be a small extriangulated category.
Let
\delta\in\mathbb{E}(C,A)
be an
\mathbb{E}
-extension.
Take a realization
A\xrightarrow{x}B\xrightarrow{y}C
of
\delta

and define
\widetilde{\delta}
to be the cokernel of
\mathcal{C}(?,y):\mathcal{C}(?,B)\rightarrow\mathcal{C}(?,C)
in
\mathrm{Mod}\,\mathcal{C}
.
The functor
\widetilde{\delta}
is called the defect of
\delta
,
or the defect of an
\mathfrak{s}
-conflation
A\xrightarrow{x}B\xrightarrow{y}C
.
We denote by
\mathrm{def}\kern 1.00006pt\mathbb{E}
the subcategory of
\mathrm{Mod}\,\mathcal{C}
consisting of
\mathcal{C}
-modules which are isomorphic to defects of some
\mathfrak{s}
-conflations.
