A diagram chasing shows that there is a short exact sequence of defects

0\rightarrow\widetilde{\theta}\rightarrow\widetilde{\delta}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mu}\rightarrow 0

Theorem 4.32

(
[
10
]
, Theorem B)
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
be a small extriangulated category.
Then the map
\mathbb{F}\mapsto\mathrm{def}\kern 1.00006pt\mathbb{F}
defines an isomorphism between the following posets, where the poset structures are given by inclusion.

(1)

The poset of closed subbifunctors of
\mathbb{E}
.

(2)

The poset of Serre subcategories of
\mathrm{def}\kern 1.00006pt\mathbb{E}
.

Theorem 4.33
.

Let
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})
be an exact dg category and
(H^{0}(\mathcal{A}),\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
the associated algebraic extriangulated category. The following posets, where the poset structures are given by inclusion, are isomorphic.

(1)

The poset of exact substructures of
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})
.

(2)

The poset of closed subbifunctors of
\mathbb{E}
.

(3)

The poset of Serre subcategories of
\mathrm{def}\kern 1.00006pt\mathbb{E}
.

Proof.

The isomorphism between items (2) and (3) is given by Theorem
4.32
.
We show the isomorphism between items (1) and (2).

Let
\mathbb{F}\subset\mathbb{E}
be a closed subbifunctor.
Let
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{F}}\subseteq\mathcal{S}
be the class of conflations
X

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

whose equivalence class
[X]\in\mathbb{F}(C,A)\subseteq\mathbb{E}(C,A)
.
We first show that the class
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{F}}
is closed under isomorphisms.
Let
X
be a conflation in
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{F}}
and
\alpha:X\rightarrow X^{\prime}
an isomorphism in
\mathcal{H}_{3t}(\mathcal{A})
.
Then
X^{\prime}
is a conflation in
\mathcal{S}
.
By Lemma
4.10
, the isomorphism
\alpha
factorises as the composition of isomorphisms
X\rightarrow\widetilde{X}
and
\widetilde{X}\rightarrow X^{\prime}
and
[\widetilde{X}]=\overline{a}_{*}[X]=\overline{c}^{*}[X^{\prime}]
for some isomorphisms
\overline{a}
and
\overline{c}
in
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
.
So we have
[X^{\prime}]=(\overline{c}^{-1})^{*}\overline{a}_{*}[X]
and
X^{\prime}
also belongs to
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{F}}
.
Since
\mathbb{F}
is an additive subbifunctor, Axiom
\mathrm{Ex}0
for
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{F}}
is satisfied.
By Proposition
4.29
,
\mathfrak{s}|_{\mathbb{F}}
-deflations are closed under compositions.
Since we have showed
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{F}}
is closed under isomorphisms, deflations are closed under compositions.
Thus Axiom
{\mathrm{Ex}1}
is satisfied.
Suppose we are in the context of Axiom
\mathrm{Ex}2
.
So we have a cospan

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

where
p:B\rightarrow C
is a deflation in
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{F}}
.
Put
[X^{\prime}]=\overline{c}^{*}[X]
.
The morphism from
X^{\prime}
to
X
then restricts to the homotopy pullback of the above cospan.
Since
X^{\prime}
also belongs to
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{F}}
, the homotopy pullback of
p
is also a deflation in
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{F}}
.
Thus Axiom
{\mathrm{Ex}2}
is satisfied.
Dually, one shows Axiom
{\mathrm{Ex}2^{op}}
for
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{F}}
.

Let
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{S}
be an exact dg substructure.
Let
(H^{0}(\mathcal{A}),\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}},\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})
be the corresponding extriangulated structure.
The inclusion
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{S}
induces a natural transformation of additive bifunctors
\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}\rightarrow\mathbb{E}
which is compatible with the realizations
\mathfrak{s}
and
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}
.
By Proposition
4.19
, the natural transformation
\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}\rightarrow\mathbb{E}
is an inclusion and thus defines a closed subbifunctor of
\mathbb{E}
by Proposition
4.29
.

It is direct to check that the maps
\mathbb{F}\mapsto\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{F}}
and
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\mapsto\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}
are inverse to each other.
∎

Corollary 4.34
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
be an algebraic extriangulated category.
Let
\mathbb{F}\subset\mathbb{E}
be a closed subbifunctor.
Then the extriangulated category
(\mathcal{C},\mathbb{F},\mathfrak{s}|_{\mathbb{F}})
is still an algebraic extriangulated category.

Remark 4.35
.

We have an injection

\{\text{Exact dg structures on $\mathcal{A}$}\}\to\{\text{Extriangulated structures on $H^{0}(\mathcal{A})$}\}.

This map is in general not a surjection.
For example, when
\mathcal{A}
is concentrated in degree
0
,
then the exact dg structures on
\mathcal{A}
are exactly the Quillen exact structures on
\mathcal{A}
.
But
\mathcal{A}
may have nontrivial extriangulated structures, e.g. if
\mathcal{A}
is an algebraic triangulated category.

Definition 4.36

(
[
18
]
, Definitions 2.1, 2.7)
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
be an extriangulated category.
A non-split (i.e. non zero)
\mathbb{E}
-extension
\delta\in\mathbb{E}(C,A)
is said to be almost split if it satisfies the following conditions.

(AS1)

a_{*}(\delta)=0
for any non-section
a\in\mathcal{C}(A,A^{\prime})
.

(AS2)

c^{*}(\delta)=0
for any non-retraction
c\in\mathcal{C}(C^{\prime},C)
.

A sequence of morphisms
A\xrightarrow{x}B\xrightarrow{y}C
in
\mathcal{C}
is called an almost split sequence if it realizes some almost split extension
\delta\in\mathbb{E}(C,A)
.

A non-zero object
A\in\mathcal{C}
is
endo-local
if
\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)
is local.
For any almost split
\mathbb{E}
-extension
\delta\in\mathbb{E}(C,A)
, we have that
A
and
C
are both endo-local, cf.
[
18
, Proposition 2.5]
.
Almost split extensions are unique in the following sense.

Proposition 4.37

(
[
18
]
, Proposition 2.6)
.

Let
A\in\mathcal{C}
be any object. If there are non-split extensions
\delta\in\mathbb{E}(C,A)
and
\delta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{E}(C^{\prime},A)
satisfying (AS2), then there is an isomorphism
c\in\mathcal{C}(C^{\prime},C)
such that
c^{*}(\delta)=\delta^{\prime}
.
Dually, if non-split extensions
\rho\in\mathbb{E}(C,A)
and
\rho^{\prime}\in\mathbb{E}(C,A^{\prime})
satisfy (AS1) for some
A
,
A^{\prime}
,
C\in\mathcal{C}
, then there is an isomorphism
a\in\mathcal{C}(A,A^{\prime})
such that
a_{*}(\rho)=\rho^{\prime}
.

Definition 4.38

(
[
18
]
, Definition 3.1)
.

We say that
\mathcal{C}
has right almost split extensions if for any endo-local non-projective object
A\in\mathcal{C}
, there exists an almost split extension
\delta\in\mathbb{E}(A,B)
for some
B\in\mathcal{C}
.
Dually, we say that
\mathcal{C}
has left almost split extensions if if for any endo-local non-injective object
B\in\mathcal{C}
, there exists an almost split extension
\delta\in\mathbb{E}(A,B)
for some
A\in\mathcal{C}
.
We say that
\mathcal{C}
has almost split extensions if it has right and left almost split extensions.

Let
k
be a field. Suppose
\mathcal{C}
is Krull–Schmidt.
Let
A\xrightarrow{x}B\xrightarrow{y}C
be a conflation realizing an almost split
\mathbb{E}
-extension
\delta\in\mathbb{E}(C,A)
.
Then its defect
\widetilde{\delta}
, i.e. the cokernel of
\mathcal{C}(?,y):\mathcal{C}(?,B)\rightarrow\mathcal{C}(?,C)
in
\mathrm{Mod}\,\mathcal{C}
, is the simple module at
C
.
So we have
\widetilde{\delta}(C)=\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(C)/\mathrm{rad}\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(C)
, and
\widetilde{\delta}(D)=0
for any indecomposable
D\in\mathcal{C}
that is not isomorphic to
C
.
Conversely, if a simple module
S_{C}
lies in
\mathrm{def}\kern 1.00006pt\mathbb{E}
for an indecomposable object
C\in\mathcal{C}
, then, by definition, there is a defect
\delta\in\mathbb{E}(C,A)
such that
\widetilde{\delta}
is isomorphic to
S_{C}
.
It is clear that
\delta
satisfies (AS2).
By
[
18
, Lemma 2.15]
, we may assume that
A
is indecomposable, and then
\delta
is an almost split extension, whose defect is necessarily isomorphic to
S_{C}
.
The following corollary generalizes an analogous result in exact categories, cf.
[
11
, Theorem 2.26]
.

Corollary 4.39
.

Let
k
be a field.
Let
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})
be an exact dg category such that the following conditions hold

1)

The
k
-category
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
is Krull–Schmidt.

2)

The extriangulated category
(H^{0}(\mathcal{A}),\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
is admissible, i.e. every object in
\mathrm{def}\kern 1.00006pt\mathbb{E}
has finite length (cf.
[
9
]
).

Then the lattice of exact substructures of
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})
is isomorphic to the Boolean lattice of the subsets of the set of isomorphism classes of indecomposable non-projective objects in
(H^{0}(\mathcal{A}),\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a set of representatives for each isomorphism class of non-projective indecomposable objects in
\mathcal{C}=H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
.
Let us show that for each
C\in\mathcal{M}
, there exists an almost split extension with ending term
C
.
Indeed, since
C
is non-projective and
\mathcal{C}
is Krull–Schmidt, there exists a non-zero extension
\delta\in\mathbb{E}(C,A)
with
A
indecomposable.
By item 2), each defect has finite length. Let us choose
\delta
such that the length of
\widetilde{\delta}
is minimal among such extensions.
Let
c:C^{\prime}\rightarrow C
be a non-retraction morphism in
\mathcal{C}
.
Let us consider the diagram (
4.31.1
) in Remark
4.31
.
So there is a surjection from
\widetilde{\delta}
to
\widetilde{\mu}
where
\mu=u_{*}(\delta)
for some morphism
u:A\rightarrow E
.
Since
C
is indecomposable and
\delta
is non-zero and
c
is a non-retraction, the
\mathbb{E}
-extension
\mu
is nonzero.
We decompose
E
into indecomposables
E=\oplus_{i}E_{i}
.
Then for some
p_{i}:E\rightarrow E_{i}
, we have
\mu_{i}=(p_{i})_{*}(\mu)\neq 0
.
There is a surjection
\widetilde{\mu}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mu_{i}}
. So the composition
\widetilde{\delta}\twoheadrightarrow\widetilde{\mu}\twoheadrightarrow\widetilde{\mu_{i}}
is an isomorphism by the minimality of the length of
\widetilde{\delta}
.
Then
\widetilde{\delta}
is isomorphic to
\widetilde{\mu}
and hence
\widetilde{\theta}=0
.
It follows that
c^{*}(\theta)=0
and hence we have that
\widetilde{\delta}
is isomorphic to
S_{C}
and that
\delta
is an almost split extension.

We choose an almost split extension
\delta_{C}
for each
C\in\mathcal{M}
and put
\mathcal{R}=\{\delta_{C},C\in\mathcal{M}\}
.
Since any defect takes zero value at each projective object in
\mathcal{C}
,
the category
\mathrm{def}\kern 1.00006pt\mathbb{E}
can be identified with a full subcategory of
\mathrm{Mod}\,(\mathrm{add}\mathcal{M})
.
The defects
\widetilde{\delta_{C}}
are the simple modules in
\mathrm{Mod}\,(\mathrm{add}\mathcal{M})
and hence we have that
\mathrm{def}\kern 1.00006pt\mathbb{E}
is the full subcategory of
\mathrm{Mod}\,(\mathrm{add}\mathcal{M})
consisting of objects which have finite lengths.
Recall that in a small finite length abelian category, the lattice of Serre subcategories is isomorphic to the lattice of the subsets of the set of isomorphism classes of simple objects.
Therefore, by Theorem
4.33
, the lattice of exact substructures of
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})
is isomorphic to the Boolean lattice of the subsets of the set of isomorphism classes of indecomposable non-projective objects in
\mathcal{C}
.
∎

5.
Example

In this section, we illustrate our theory of exact dg categories using a typical class of examples: dg categories of two-term complexes of finitely generated projective modules over finite-dimensional algebras.
We demonstrate, by providing explicit examples, that there exist short exact sequences
which do not belong to the exact structure inherited from the ambient pretriangulated dg categories.
Furthermore, we offer a concrete example of a (hereditary!) algebra where the lattice of exact structures is given explicitly.

5.1.
Homotopy category of two-term complexes

Throughout this section, let
k
be a field and
A
a finite-dimensional
k
-algebra.
Let
\mathcal{H}^{[-1,0]}(\mathrm{proj}\,A)
be the full subcategory of
\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{H}^{b}(\mathrm{proj}\,A)
consisting of two-term complexes
P^{-1}\to P^{0}
of finitely generated projective
A
-modules, where
P^{i}
is concentrated in degree
i
.
Let
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{C}^{b}_{dg}(\mathrm{proj}\,A)
be the canonical enhancement of

{\mathcal{H}}^{b}(\mathrm{proj}\,A)
and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
its full dg subcategory on the objects
P^{-1}\to P^{0}
.
Then
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is stable under extensions in the pretriangulated dg category
\mathcal{A}
and thus inherits a canonical exact dg structure.
For an
A
-module
M
, we denote by
M^{*}
its
A
-dual.

Proposition 5.1
.

There exists a homotopy short exact sequence in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
which is not homotopy short exact in

\mathcal{A}
if and only if there exists an
A
-module
M
satisfying the following properties

(1)

\mathrm{pd}_{A}M=1
, and

(2)

\mathrm{pd}_{A^{op}}M^{*}=1
, and

(3)

M
is reflexive, i.e. the canonical map
M\rightarrow M^{**}
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Suppose we are given such an
A
-module
M
.
We consider a minimal projective presentation of
M

0\rightarrow P_{1}\rightarrow P_{0}\rightarrow M\rightarrow 0.

By taking the
A
-dual, we obtain a left exact sequence of left
A
-modules

0\rightarrow M^{*}\rightarrow P_{0}^{*}\rightarrow P_{1}^{*}.

Put
N=M^{*}
. We then take a minimal projective presentation of
N

0\rightarrow Q_{1}\rightarrow Q_{0}\rightarrow N\rightarrow 0.

Again, by taking the
A
-dual, we arrive at a left exact sequence of right
A
-modules

0\rightarrow M\rightarrow Q_{0}^{*}\rightarrow Q_{1}^{*},

since
M
is assumed to be reflexive.
The composition
P_{0}\rightarrow M\rightarrow Q_{0}^{*}
then yields the following
3
-term homotopy complex

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(P_{1}\rightarrow P_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0\rightarrow Q_{0}^{*})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0\rightarrow Q_{1}^{*})}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

which is homotopy short exact in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
, but not in
\mathcal{A}
.

Suppose we have a
3
-term homotopy complex in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}

(5.1.1)

\hbox to195.45pt{\vbox to46.1pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 97.72334pt\lower-35.3687pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{52.76869pt}{3.71385pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

We form the following diagram in
\mathcal{A}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q^{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U=\mathrm{Cone}(f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma P^{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V=\Sigma^{-1}\mathrm{Cone}(g)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q^{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[}1{,}0{]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[}-1{,}0{]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}}

where
u={[}-h{,}g{]}
,
v=\begin{bmatrix}-f\\
-h\end{bmatrix}
and
s=\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
-1&0\end{bmatrix}
.
By definition, the
3
-term homotopy complex (
5.1.1
) is homotopy short exact if and only if, for any
S^{\bullet}\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
, the maps
u
and
v
induce canonical quasi-isomorphisms of complexes

(5.1.2)

\tau_{\leq 0}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(S^{\bullet},P^{\bullet})\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\tau_{\leq 0}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(S^{\bullet},V),

and

(5.1.3)

\tau_{\leq 0}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(R^{\bullet},S^{\bullet})\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\tau_{\leq 0}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(U,S^{\bullet}).

We observe that the formula (
5.1.2
) holds for any
S^{\bullet}\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
if and only if it holds for
S^{\bullet}=A
if and only if
P^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\tau_{\leq 0}V
.
Similarly, the formula (
5.1.3
) holds for any
S^{\bullet}\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
if and only if it holds for
S^{\bullet}=\Sigma A
if and only if
\mathrm{Hom}(R^{\bullet},A)\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\tau_{\leq 0}\mathrm{Hom}(U,A)
.
Put
M=\mathrm{ker}(Q^{0}\oplus R^{-1}\xrightarrow{[-g^{0},-d]}R^{0})
.
Then the 3-term homotopy complex (
5.1.1
) is homotopy left exact if and only if the following is a projective presentation of
M

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{0}\oplus Q^{-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[}d{,}f^{-1}{]^{\intercal}}}

In other words, the morphism
g:Q^{\bullet}\rightarrow R^{\bullet}
admits a homotopy kernel if and only if
\mathrm{pd}_{A}M\leq 1
.

Now, we suppose that the
3
-term homotopy complex (
5.1.1
) is homotopy short exact in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
.
Then, by the observation made earlier, we have
\mathrm{pd}_{A}(M)\leq 1
.
By taking the
A
-dual, we see that
M^{*}\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathrm{ker}((P^{0})^{*}\oplus(Q^{-1})^{*}\rightarrow(P^{-1})^{*})
and the following is a projective presentation of
M^{*}

(5.1.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(R^{0})^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(R^{-1})^{*}\oplus(Q^{0})^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}

Hence, we also have
\mathrm{pd}_{A^{op}}(M^{*})\leq 1
.
By taking the
A
-dual of the sequence (
5.1.4
) and by the definition of
M
, we see that the canonical morphism
M\rightarrow M^{**}
is an isomorphism.
Therefore, the
A
-module
M
is reflexive.
If the left
A
-module
M^{*}
is projective, then the sequence (
5.1.4
) splits and hence the morphism
Q^{0}\oplus R^{-1}\xrightarrow{[-g^{0},-d]}R^{0}
is a surjection.
It follows that
V
is quasi-isomorphic to
\tau_{\leq 0}V
and the
3
-term homotopy complex (
5.1.1
) is homotopy short exact in
\mathcal{A}
.

∎

We give examples of algebras
A
admitting such modules.
Note that such an algebra can neither be hereditary, nor local nor self-injective.
Consider the algebras given by the quivers with relations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 3,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 5}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 4}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 3}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 6.}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 3}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 4,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 4}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 3}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 5,}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 3}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 4.}

Then the simple modules
S_{2}
at the vertices 2 satisfy the above properties.

Example 5.2
.

Let
A
be the path
k
-algebra
k(\overrightarrow{A_{2}})
of the quiver
A_{2}
.
As the algebra
A
is hereditary, the inherited exact structure on
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is its greatest exact dg structure, as can be seen by Proposition
5.1
.
Let
(\mathcal{C}=H^{0}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}),\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
be the corresponding extriangulated category.
We denote by
S_{2}
the two-term complex
P_{1}\rightarrow P_{2}
where
P_{i}
is the indecomposable projective
A
-module corresponding to the vertex
i
.
Then the AR-quiver of
\mathcal{C}
is as follows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma P_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma P_{2}.}

We take the following AR-sequences:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha:P_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma P_{1},}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta:P_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{2},}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma:S_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma P_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma P_{2}.}

By Corollary
4.39
, the lattice of exact structures on
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is isomorphic to the following Boolean lattice:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\alpha,\beta,\gamma\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\alpha,\beta\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\beta,\gamma\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\alpha,\gamma\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\alpha\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\beta\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\gamma\}.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\emptyset}

6.
Stable dg categories

In this section, we focus on a specific class of additive dg categories, the class of
stable dg categories
.
The property of being stable is invariant under isomorphisms in the localization
\mathrm{Hqe}_{\leq 0}
of
\mathrm{dgcat}
at the connective quasi-equivalences, as defined in Definition
2.1
.
Typical examples include (connective covers of) pretriangulated dg categories.
For a Jacobi-finite quiver with potential
(Q,W)
, we observe that
\mathcal{F}_{dg}
is stable, where
\mathcal{F}_{dg}\subseteq\mathrm{per}_{dg}(\Gamma_{Q,W})
is the canonical dg enhancement of the
fundamental domain

[
1
, 2.2]

\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathrm{per}(\Gamma_{Q,W})
.
This provides a class of non-connective dg categories that are stable but not pretriangulated.

6.1.
Stable dg categories

We have the following key notion of this section. It is a dg analog of Lurie’s notion of stable
\infty
-category
[
32
, Definition 1.1.1.9]
.

Definition 6.1
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an additive dg category.
It is stable if the following conditions are satisfied:

(a)

the dg category
\mathcal{A}
admits homotopy kernels and homotopy cokernels;

(b)

a 3-term homotopy complex in
\mathcal{A}

is homotopy left exact if and only if it is homotopy right exact.

It is straightforward to check that the property of being stable is invariant under connective quasi-equivalences, cf. Definition
2.1
.

Example 6.2
.

By Example
4.7
, a pretriangulated dg category
\mathcal{A}
, and hence its connective cover
\tau_{\leq 0}\mathcal{A}
, is stable.

Remark 6.3
.

The notion of stable dg category is more flexible than the notion of pretriangulated dg category, in the sense that it is preserved under connective quasi-equivalences, which is not the case at all for the notion of pretriangulated dg category. In a subsequent paper, we will show that they are essentially the same notion, in the sense that each stable dg category is isomorphic to a pretriangulated dg category in
\mathrm{Hqe}_{\leq 0}
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a stable dg category. It is clear that the following
3
-term homotopy complex in
\mathcal{A}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}

is homotopy short exact. Since the dg category
\mathcal{A}
admits homotopy pullbacks which are homotopy bicartesian, each morphism in
Z^{0}(\mathcal{A})
is a deflation. Dually, each morphism in
Z^{0}(\mathcal{A})
is an inflation. Thus, it is straightforward to verify the following proposition.

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a stable dg category and
\mathcal{S}
the class of all homotopy short exact sequences in
\mathcal{A}
. Then
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})
is an exact dg category.

Theorem 6.5
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a small exact dg category.
Then the extriangulated structure on
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
by Theorem
4.26
is triangulated if and only if
\mathcal{A}
is a stable dg category with the canonical exact structure.

Proof.

According to
[
35
, Corollary 7.6]
, an extriangulated category is tringulated if and only if it is a Frobenius extriangulated category with the full subcategory of projective-injectives being zero.

Assume the dg category
\mathcal{A}
is stable with the canonical exact structure.
Let
A
be an object in
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
. By the definition of stability, it admits conflations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A,}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime\prime}.}

Hence, the extriangulated category
(H^{0}(\mathcal{A}),\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
is Frobenius with the full subcategory of projective-injectives being zero.
Therefore, it is a triangulated category.

Conversely, let us assume that the extriangulated category
(H^{0}(\mathcal{A}),\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
is Frobenius with the full subcategory of projective-injectives being zero.
So for any object
A\in\mathcal{A}
, the morphism
A\rightarrow 0
is an inflation.
By the dual of Claim 1 in the proof of Proposition
4.9
, we deduce that any morphism
f:A\rightarrow B
is an inflation.
In particular, the dg category
\mathcal{A}
admits homotopy cokernels which are homotopy short exact.
Dually, it admits homotopy kernels which are homotopy short exact.
It follows that a
3
-term homotopy complex in
\mathcal{A}
is homotopy left exact if and only if it is homotopy right exact.

∎

6.2.
Amiot’s construction of fundamental domain

The following example is taken from
[
1
]
.
It serves as an illustration of non-connective dg categories that are stable but not pretriangulated.
Recall that a dg functor
F:\mathcal{F}_{dg}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{dg}
between pretriangulated dg categories necessarily induces a triangle functor
H^{0}(F):H^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{dg})\rightarrow H^{0}(\mathcal{C}_{dg})
.
It also demonstrates that a dg functor between stable dg categories does not necessarily induce a triangle functor.

Let
k
be a field. Recall that a dg
k
-algebra
A
is
homologically smooth
if the object
A
, viewed as an
A^{e}
-module, is perfect.
It is
connective
if for each
p>0
, the space
H^{p}(A)
vanishes. It is
bimodule
3
-CY
if there is an isomorphism in
\mathcal{D}(A^{e})

R\mathrm{Hom}_{A^{e}}(A,A^{e})\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\Sigma^{-3}A.

Let
A
be a homologically smooth, connective, bimodule
3
-CY dg
k
-algebra, such that
H^{0}(A)
is finite-dimensional.
Note that the opposite dg algebra
A^{op}
also satisfies these properties.
For example, one could take the Ginzburg dg algebra
\Gamma_{Q,W}
associated with a Jacobi-finite quiver with potential
(Q,W)
.
Let
\mathrm{per}(A)
be the perfect derived category of
A
, which is the thick subcategory of the derived category
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}(A)
generated by
A
.
A dg
A\mbox{-}
module is
perfectly valued
if its total cohomology is finite-dimensional and we denote by
\mathrm{pvd}(A)
the triangulated category of perfectly valued dg
A\mbox{-}
modules.
By
[
26
, Lemma 4.1]
,
\mathrm{pvd}(A)
is a full subcategory of
\mathrm{per}(A)
and we have a bifunctorial isomorphism

D\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}}(L,M)\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}}(M,\Sigma^{3}L)

for all objects
L
in
\mathrm{pvd}(A)
and all objects
M
in
\mathcal{D}(A)
.
Recall the canonical t-structure
(\mathcal{D}^{\leq 0},\mathcal{D}^{\geq 0})
on
\mathcal{D}
, cf. Lemma
2.9
, where
\mathcal{D}^{\leq 0}
(resp.
\mathcal{D}^{\geq 0}
) is the full subcategory consisting of dg
A
-modules whose cohomology is concentrated in non-positive (resp. non-negative) degrees.
We denote
\tau_{\leq 0}
as the associated truncation functor. Note that on a dg
A
-module
M
, the underlying complex of the truncation
\tau_{\leq 0}M
is obtained by applying the truncation
\tau_{\leq 0}
to the underlying complex of
M
, while preserving the induced
A
-action.
The
fundamental domain
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}({A})

[
1
, 2.2]
is defined as

\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{D}^{\leq 0}\cap{{}^{\perp}\mathcal{D}^{\leq-2}}\cap\mathrm{per}(A),

where
{}^{\perp}(-)
denotes the Hom orthogonal.
Put
\mathcal{P}=\mathrm{add}(A)
.
By
[
1
, Lemma 2.10]
, it can also be described as
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{P}\ast\Sigma\mathcal{P}
.
Let
\mathcal{C}
be the Verdier quotient
\mathrm{per}(A)/\mathrm{pvd}(A)
.
By
[
1
, Proposition 2.9]
, the projection functor
\pi:\mathrm{per}(A)\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
induces a
k
-linear equivalence

\pi:\mathcal{F}\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathcal{C}.

We add the subscript
dg
to the aforementioned categories to denote their dg enhancements.
In particular,
\mathcal{C}_{dg}
is the dg quotient
\mathrm{per}_{dg}(A)/\mathrm{pvd}_{dg}(A)
.
Since
\mathcal{F}_{dg}\subset\mathrm{per}_{dg}(A)
is not stable under shifts, it is not a pretriangulated dg category.
The following proposition provides a dg enhancement of the above
k
-linear equivalence.
In particular, it implies that the dg category
\mathcal{F}_{dg}
is stable.

Proposition 6.6
.

The dg quotient functor
\mathrm{per}_{dg}(\mathcal{A})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{dg}
induces a connective quasi-equivalence between
\mathcal{F}_{dg}
and
\mathcal{C}_{dg}
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that for objects
X
and
Y
in
\mathcal{F}
and
i\leq 0
, the quotient functor
\pi:\mathrm{per}(A)\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
induces a canonical isomorphism

(6.6.1)

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}(A)}(X,\Sigma^{i}Y)\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,\Sigma^{i}Y).

We begin by showing that the map (
6.6.1
) is an injection.
Suppose
f:X\rightarrow\Sigma^{i}Y
is a morphism in
\mathrm{per}(A)
that factors through an object
M
in
\mathrm{pvd}(A)
. Since
X\in\mathcal{D}^{\leq 0}
, we may assume that
M\in\mathrm{pvd}(A)^{\leq 0}
. Then, we have

D\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}(A)}(M,\Sigma^{i}Y)\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}(A)}(\Sigma^{i}Y,\Sigma^{3}M)=0

since
Y\in{{}^{\perp}\mathcal{D}^{\leq-2}}
. Consequently, the morphism
f
is also zero and the map (
6.6.1
) is an injection.
Now, let us demonstrate the surjectivity of the map (
6.6.1
).
Consider a roof
b/s

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{i}Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

where
\mathrm{Cone}(s)\in\mathrm{pvd}(A)
.
Since
X\in\mathrm{per}(A)^{\leq 0}
,we may assume that
\mathrm{Cone}(s)\in\mathrm{pvd}(A)^{\leq 0}
.
Then we have

D\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}(A)}(\Sigma^{-1}\mathrm{Cone}(s),\Sigma^{i}Y)\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}(A)}(\Sigma^{i}Y,\Sigma^{2}\mathrm{Cone}(s))=0

since
Y\in{{}^{\perp}\mathcal{D}^{\leq-2}}
. Thus, the morphism
b
factors through the morphism
s
and this shows the surjectivity of the map (
6.6.1
).
∎

Our next aim is to describe the homotopy short exact sequences in
\mathcal{F}_{dg}
.
For this, we use the quasi-equivalence
\Sigma\mathrm{RHom}_{A}(-,A):\mathcal{F}_{dg}^{op}\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathcal{F}_{dg}(A^{op})
, and it suffices to describe homotopy kernels in
\mathcal{F}_{dg}
.
By
[
1
, Lemma 2.5]
, the space
H^{p}(A)
is finite-dimensional for each
p
.
Consequently, the triangulated category
\mathrm{per}(A)
is Hom-finite, and the t-structure
(\mathcal{D}^{\leq 0},\mathcal{D}^{\geq 0})
restricts to a t-structure
(\mathrm{per}(A)^{\leq 0},\mathrm{per}(A)^{\geq 0})
on
\mathrm{per}(A)
.
Now, let us elucidate the homotopy kernels of morphisms in
\mathcal{F}_{dg}(A)
.
Let
g:M\rightarrow N
be a morphism in
Z^{0}\mathcal{F}_{dg}(A)
.
Define
L=\Sigma^{-1}\mathrm{Cone}(g)
as its homotopy kernel in
\mathrm{per}_{dg}(A)
.
So we have the following homotopy short exact sequence in
\mathrm{per}_{dg}(A)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

Lemma 6.7
.

The truncation
\tau_{\leq 0}L
lies in
\mathcal{F}
and the inclusion
i:\tau_{\leq 0}L\rightarrow L
yields a homotopy short exact sequence in
\mathcal{F}_{dg}(A)

(6.7.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{\leq 0}L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

where
f=f^{\prime}i
and
h=h^{\prime}i
.

Proof.

It is evident that
L
, as an extension of
\Sigma^{-1}N
and
M
, belongs to
{{}^{\perp}\mathcal{D}^{\leq-2}}
.
Furthermore, it is clear that
L\in\mathcal{D}^{\leq 1}
, indicating that
\tau_{\geq 1}L
lies in the shift of the heart of t-structure on
\mathrm{per}(A)
. Specifically, we have
\tau_{\geq 1}L\in\mathrm{pvd}(A)
.
Thus, for each
M\in{\mathcal{D}^{\leq-2}}
, we have

D\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}}(\Sigma^{-1}\tau_{\geq 1}L,M)\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}}(M,\Sigma^{2}\tau_{\geq 1}L)=0.

This implies that
\tau_{\leq 0}L
, as an extension of
\Sigma^{-1}\tau_{\geq 1}L
and
L
, belongs to
{{}^{\perp}\mathcal{D}^{\leq-2}}
.
Therefore,
\tau_{\leq 0}L
lies in
\mathcal{F}
.

Now, let us verify that the homotopy 3-term complex (
6.7.1
) is homotopy short exact in
\mathcal{F}_{dg}(A)
.
Indeed, let
K\in\mathcal{F}
and
i\leq 0
.
Then
\mathrm{Hom}(K,\Sigma^{i}\tau_{\geq 1}L)=0
and

D\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}(A)}(\tau_{\geq 1}L,\Sigma^{i}K)\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}(A)}(\Sigma^{i}K,\Sigma^{3}\tau_{\geq 1}L)=0.

Thus, the canonical map
\tau_{\leq 0}L\rightarrow L
induces an isomorphism

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}(A)}(K,\Sigma^{i}\tau_{\leq 0}L)\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}(A)}(K,\Sigma^{i}L).

The canonical morphism
\mathrm{Cone}(f)\rightarrow N
induces an isomorphism

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}(A)}(N,\Sigma^{i}K)\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}(A)}(\mathrm{Cone}(f),\Sigma^{i}K).

This confirms that (
6.7.1
) is homotopy short exact.
∎

Example 6.8
.

In the special case where
(Q,W)
is given by the quiver
A_{1}
with a trivial potential
W=0
,
the associated Ginzburg dg algebra
\Gamma_{Q,W}
is the polynomial algebra
k[t]
, where
t
has degree
-2
.
Consequently, the associated fundamental domain
\mathcal{F}
is equivalent, as a
k
-category, to the category
\mathrm{Vect}^{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}}
of finite-dimensional super vector spaces
V=V_{0}\oplus V_{1}
.
The category
\mathrm{Vect}^{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}}
admits a canonical dg enhancement
\mathrm{Vect}^{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}}_{dg}
: for any two objects
V
and
V^{\prime}
in
\mathrm{Vect}^{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}}
, the Hom complex
\mathrm{Hom}(V,V^{\prime})
is defined such that

\mathrm{Hom}^{2n}(V,V^{\prime})=\mathrm{Hom}_{k}(V_{1},V_{1}^{\prime})\oplus\mathrm{Hom}_{k}(V_{2},V_{2}^{\prime}),

\mathrm{Hom}^{2n-1}(V,V^{\prime})=\mathrm{Hom}_{k}(V_{1},V_{2}^{\prime})\oplus\mathrm{Hom}_{k}(V_{2},V_{1}^{\prime}),

for
n\leq 0
, and

\mathrm{Hom}^{1}(V,V^{\prime})=\mathrm{Hom}_{k}(V_{1},V_{2}^{\prime}),\quad\mathrm{Hom}^{\geq 2}(V,V^{\prime})=0,

and the differential is trivial.
By the results presented in this subsection, we obtain that the dg category
\mathrm{Vect}^{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}}_{dg}
is stable. Its homotopy kernels and homotopy cokernels are given as follows. Let
f:V\rightarrow V^{\prime}
be a morphism of super vector spaces.
Its homotopy kernel is

\hbox to200.4pt{\vbox to41.77pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 100.19978pt\lower-30.27527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
